PREFACE.
The printed text of the Jataka-mala is based upon the follow-
ing Manuscripts
A: Add. MS. 1328 of University Library, Cambridge.
B: Add. , 1415 x $ B
P: MS. 95 Burnouf, Bibl. Nationale, Paris.
For the two Cambridge MSS. I refer to the Catalogue of Bud-
dhist Sanskrit Manuscripts in the University Library, Cambridge, by
Cecil Bendall, p. 51 and p. 92; about the Paris MS. see L. Feer, in
Journal Asiatique, May 1875, p. 413 (').
These MSS. are all of them derived from one and the same,
somewhat remote, source. This is proved by their having in
common a great many striking errors; ९ 18, 1. 10 all omit
दान; 1. 20, 1. 11 all have 'टिसन्ति for दित्सन्तिः p- 21, L 2 JAT-
न्ति० for तच्छान्ति०; ए: 38, | 20 अनघे for अर्धे; ए. 52, 1. 15 प्रभत
for NAA; 7. 72, L 1 gR: for यशः: 1. 13 सध्यापार for सव्यापार
p. 107, 1. 1 निरसस्बस्‌ for निवसस्वम:ः etc. Another not less coun-
clusive argument is afforded by the circumstance that the MSS
agree in having no indication of the number of glokas (al. granthas
of 32 syllables) before the end of Jat, xviii, nor after Jat. xxi, xxii
and xxxiv, whereas such an indication reappears Jat. xxiii—xxxüi.
Notwithstanding their proceeding from one archetype, which,
as the instances adduced abundantly prove, must have been far
(१ A MS. in Nagari of the Bibliothèque Nationale (s. Feer L c.) and another
jn St. Petersburg appear from some test passages to be mere copies from B, or
from its original; s. Bendall Catalogue p. 93 and cp. the remark by S. Oldenburg in
Zapiski Vostocknago Otdeleniya Imper. Russkago Archevlog. Obshchesiva IV, p. 384.

vi Preface.
from correct, the MSS. show several discrepancies of such a kind
as cannot have their origin in clerical errors. This fact, however,
is easily accounted for. Our MSS. contain a considerable number
of second hand or marginal readings, partly, it may be assumed,
various readings taken from Codices of another family, partly
probably conjectures added by some reader or corrector. Here
and there we see how the scribes, by mistake, have mixed up two
various readings. So e. g. P p. 13, 1. 19 has च a instead of either
ऽत्र or नु; B p. 239, 1.10 fasya न लभ्यते, where two readings
have been blended, विलुणते and ने लभ्यते. In 4 7. 91.1. 8 8
whole marginal gloss: शीता पातपटविशषः has crept into the text.
In: general it should be observed that the scribes of our MSS.
४
or their predecessors have allowed themselves some liberties with
their original, not only with regard to matters of little importance,
as spelling and punctuation, but to the choice of words. P goes
so far as to insert a whole tale, the Kacchapa-jataka, after xvi.
The spuriousness of this: Jataka, which is given in its crude state
as an Appendix, is clear at first sight, since it is written in a
totally different kind of language, something like the so-called `
Gatha-dialect. Both the Kacchapa-jataka and the next following
begin with the word aneka, and this circumstance accounts for the
place of the insertion('), but not for the addition of a whole tale, by
which the number of 34 has been exceeded. This isthe official number
of Jatakas according to the Northern Buddhists; hence one of the
epithets of the Buddha is catustrimeapatakajna(?). But the Cariya-
Titaks of the Southern canon contains 35 tales. Now it is not im-
possible that the same tradition’ lingered among some Buddhists

Kacchapa-jataka, which they may have taken from their own
collection.

As to punctuation, there is a considerable difference between B

Viil
Preface.
and the two other Codices. In B only one sign is used, the double
stroke; A and P have three signs, viz. a dot between two syllables,
a single and a double stroke. The dot is put, in verses, after the
first and third pådas; in prose, it has the value of our comma,
though its use is much more limited; it chiefly serves to separate
two attributes preceding a substantive. It should be observed,
however, that the dot is often wanting, where we should expect it.
In the printed text I have, to a certain extent, followed the system
of A and P. The distinction between single and double stroke is
also found in other Buddhist texts, as the Lalita-Vistara and the
Astasahasrika Paramita,
A discussion of the historical and literary questions connected
with the subject matter and the author of the Jataka-mali does
not lie within the scope of this preface, but, for the convenience of
the reader, I think it proper to give a comparative list of the tales
in the present work and those in the Pali collection of Jatakas,
because a comparison of the two redactions tarows light upon the ने
meaning of sundry passages. The list is incomplete, for the fifth
volume of Prof. Fausbill’s edition has not yet appeared. I have
also added the corresponding tales of the Cariya-Pitaka, edited by
the Rey. R. Morris.

So far as I know, there are only a few tales of our Jataka-
mala, which have their parallels in works not belonging to Bud-
dhist lore. The Vartaka-jataka is evidently related to the Carnga-
kopakhyana in Mabi-bhirata I, Adhy. 229 (Bombay ed). The
story of Unmidayanti is told three times over in the Kathasarit-
sagara, where the heroine is called Unmidini, s. Taramga 15,
63 ff; 33, 62 ff; 91, 3 f£. The tele of Taravaloka in the same work,
RS~E RR

x Preface.
Tar. 113, 17—95, agrees with the Vigvamtara-jataka, and in Tar,
28, 29 ff. we find the outlines of the Ksanti-jataka. In the Çata-
pattra-jataka every reader will recognize a wellknown widespread
fable, which is supposed to have its original home in ancient
Egypt.(')
It may be questioned whether there ever existed more than one
redaction or edition of our Jataka-mils. All the Manuscripts
known to us belong, indeed, to one family, but on comparing the
number of syllables according to the metrical notices at the end
of Jat. xvii etc. with the actual figures, we discover a considerable
disagreement, except in the single instance of Jat. xxiv. In this
Jat. the sum total of syllables should be 3523, and so it is really,
if we leave out of account the epilogue beginning with the stereo-
type Wea Now there is some reason to doubt the genuineness of
those epilogues, for they show a style curiously different from the
rest. It may be said that the author not unlikely followed a time-
honoured example, and on this assumption such a difference might
be explained. Internal arguments in this case not being conclu-
sive, I hoped to gather sufficient external evidence, but I have
been disappointed. The results of my enumeration compared with
the official figures partly favour the supposition of the epilogues
being spurious, partly they do not. 8686. g. we find that Jat. xxiii,
without the epilogue, contains 6440 syllables, which comes near
to 6446, the officia} number, but Jat. xviii, without the epilogue,
yields 1769, and with it 1824, which latter number remains
below the sum total according to the reckoning of the monks, viz.
1844. Under such circumstances it seemed to me that the editor
of the text had better leave the epilogues without any mark of
interpolation. | aa:
Before concluding this Preface, I have to fulfil the agreeable
task of acknowledging my obligations, in the first place, to the
authorities of Cambridge University for their having allowed me

proofsheets and by several valuable suggestions has not a little
contributed to the usefulness of my edition of the Jataka-mala.
Leiden, July 1890.
